# React Commerce Design System & Frontend Engineering Handbook

# Companion Reference Manual

# Appendix D — Enterprise CSS Variable Index & Theming Reference

---

# D.1 Introduction

CSS Variables are the runtime representation of the React Commerce Design Token System.

While Design Tokens define design decisions, CSS Variables make those decisions available to every application at runtime.

Every visual property within React Commerce should ultimately resolve to a CSS Variable.

This appendix defines the official CSS Variable architecture, naming conventions, categories, inheritance model, and theming strategy.

---

# D.2 Runtime Architecture

```text
Design Tokens

↓

Semantic Tokens

↓

CSS Variables

↓

Components

↓

Rendered UI
```

CSS Variables provide the bridge between design and implementation.

---

# D.3 Variable Naming Standard

Every variable begins with

```css
--rc-
```

Pattern

```text
--rc-category-group-name
```

Examples

```css
--rc-color-primary-500;

--rc-space-4;

--rc-radius-lg;

--rc-shadow-xl;

--rc-font-size-body;
```

Avoid abbreviations unless universally understood.

---

# D.4 Variable Categories

```text
Colors

Typography

Spacing

Radius

Borders

Shadows

Elevation

Opacity

Icons

Motion

Transitions

Breakpoints

Grid

Layout

Forms

Navigation

Charts

Workflow

AI

Collaboration
```

Variables should be grouped logically.

---

# D.5 Root Theme

```css
:root {
  color-scheme: light;

  --rc-font-family:
    Inter,
    system-ui,
    sans-serif;
}
```

The root establishes global defaults.

---

# D.6 Color Variables

Primary

```css
--rc-color-primary-50;
--rc-color-primary-100;
--rc-color-primary-200;
--rc-color-primary-300;
--rc-color-primary-400;
--rc-color-primary-500;
--rc-color-primary-600;
--rc-color-primary-700;
--rc-color-primary-800;
--rc-color-primary-900;
```

Every brand color follows the same scale.

---

# D.7 Neutral Variables

```css
--rc-color-neutral-50;

...

--rc-color-neutral-900;
```

Neutral colors form the foundation of enterprise interfaces.

---

# D.8 Semantic Colors

```css
--rc-background-default;

--rc-background-surface;

--rc-background-subtle;

--rc-background-overlay;

--rc-border-default;

--rc-border-focus;

--rc-text-primary;

--rc-text-secondary;

--rc-text-disabled;
```

Components should consume semantic variables rather than raw palette values.

---

# D.9 Status Variables

```css
--rc-success-bg;

--rc-success-text;

--rc-success-border;

--rc-warning-bg;

--rc-warning-text;

--rc-warning-border;

--rc-error-bg;

--rc-error-text;

--rc-error-border;

--rc-info-bg;

--rc-info-text;

--rc-info-border;
```

Status colors should always be available as complete sets.

---

# D.10 Typography Variables

```css
--rc-font-family-display;

--rc-font-family-body;

--rc-font-family-mono;

--rc-font-size-xs;

--rc-font-size-sm;

--rc-font-size-md;

--rc-font-size-lg;

--rc-font-size-xl;

--rc-font-weight-regular;

--rc-font-weight-medium;

--rc-font-weight-bold;
```

Typography variables should be reused across every component.

---

# D.11 Line Height Variables

```css
--rc-line-height-tight;

--rc-line-height-normal;

--rc-line-height-relaxed;

--rc-line-height-loose;
```

Maintain vertical rhythm consistently.

---

# D.12 Spacing Variables

```css
--rc-space-0;

--rc-space-1;

--rc-space-2;

--rc-space-3;

--rc-space-4;

--rc-space-5;

--rc-space-6;

--rc-space-8;

--rc-space-10;

--rc-space-12;

--rc-space-16;

--rc-space-20;

--rc-space-24;

--rc-space-32;
```

Spacing values should be the only source of layout spacing.

---

# D.13 Radius Variables

```css
--rc-radius-none;

--rc-radius-xs;

--rc-radius-sm;

--rc-radius-md;

--rc-radius-lg;

--rc-radius-xl;

--rc-radius-2xl;

--rc-radius-full;
```

Avoid hardcoded border-radius values.

---

# D.14 Border Variables

```css
--rc-border-width-none;

--rc-border-width-hairline;

--rc-border-width-sm;

--rc-border-width-md;

--rc-border-width-lg;
```

Borders should reinforce interaction and hierarchy.

---

# D.15 Shadow Variables

```css
--rc-shadow-xs;

--rc-shadow-sm;

--rc-shadow-md;

--rc-shadow-lg;

--rc-shadow-xl;

--rc-shadow-2xl;
```

Shadows indicate depth, not decoration.

---

# D.16 Elevation Variables

```css
--rc-elevation-surface;

--rc-elevation-card;

--rc-elevation-dropdown;

--rc-elevation-popover;

--rc-elevation-modal;

--rc-elevation-toast;
```

Elevation variables define stacking and visual prominence.

---

# D.17 Opacity Variables

```css
--rc-opacity-0;

--rc-opacity-25;

--rc-opacity-50;

--rc-opacity-75;

--rc-opacity-100;
```

Use predefined opacity values.

---

# D.18 Motion Variables

```css
--rc-motion-fast;

--rc-motion-normal;

--rc-motion-slow;

--rc-motion-slower;
```

Motion duration should remain predictable.

---

# D.19 Transition Variables

```css
--rc-transition-default;

--rc-transition-emphasized;

--rc-transition-opacity;

--rc-transition-transform;
```

Transitions should communicate interaction rather than decoration.

---

# D.20 Icon Variables

```css
--rc-icon-xs;

--rc-icon-sm;

--rc-icon-md;

--rc-icon-lg;

--rc-icon-xl;

--rc-icon-2xl;
```

Icon sizing should align with typography.

---

# D.21 Layout Variables

```css
--rc-header-height;

--rc-footer-height;

--rc-sidebar-width;

--rc-sidebar-collapsed-width;

--rc-page-max-width;

--rc-content-padding;
```

Layout variables define structural consistency.

---

# D.22 Grid Variables

```css
--rc-grid-columns;

--rc-grid-gap;

--rc-grid-gutter;

--rc-grid-max-width;
```

Grid values should remain consistent across layouts.

---

# D.23 Form Variables

```css
--rc-input-height-sm;

--rc-input-height-md;

--rc-input-height-lg;

--rc-input-padding;

--rc-input-border-radius;

--rc-input-border-width;
```

Forms should share a unified visual language.

---

# D.24 Button Variables

```css
--rc-button-height-sm;

--rc-button-height-md;

--rc-button-height-lg;

--rc-button-radius;

--rc-button-padding-x;

--rc-button-padding-y;
```

Buttons should derive all visual properties from variables.

---

# D.25 Navigation Variables

```css
--rc-navbar-height;

--rc-sidebar-width;

--rc-tab-height;

--rc-menu-radius;

--rc-breadcrumb-gap;
```

Navigation remains consistent across products.

---

# D.26 Table Variables

```css
--rc-table-row-height;

--rc-table-header-height;

--rc-table-cell-padding;

--rc-table-border-color;
```

Enterprise tables should share consistent metrics.

---

# D.27 Modal Variables

```css
--rc-modal-width;

--rc-modal-radius;

--rc-modal-padding;

--rc-modal-shadow;
```

Modal dimensions should remain standardized.

---

# D.28 Chart Variables

```css
--rc-chart-grid-color;

--rc-chart-axis-color;

--rc-chart-tooltip-radius;

--rc-chart-tooltip-shadow;
```

Charts inherit the same design language as the rest of the platform.

---

# D.29 Workflow Variables

```css
--rc-node-width;

--rc-node-radius;

--rc-node-gap;

--rc-edge-color;

--rc-canvas-background;
```

Workflow builders should remain theme-aware.

---

# D.30 AI Variables

```css
--rc-ai-message-radius;

--rc-ai-response-padding;

--rc-ai-avatar-size;

--rc-ai-streaming-speed;
```

AI interfaces should remain visually consistent.

---

# D.31 Collaboration Variables

```css
--rc-presence-size;

--rc-cursor-size;

--rc-comment-width;

--rc-activity-gap;
```

Realtime experiences should integrate seamlessly with the overall system.

---

# D.32 Light Theme

```css
:root {
  --rc-background-default: #ffffff;
  --rc-background-surface: #f8fafc;

  --rc-text-primary: #111827;

  --rc-border-default: #e5e7eb;
}
```

Light theme serves as the default experience.

---

# D.33 Dark Theme

```css
[data-theme="dark"] {
  --rc-background-default: #0f172a;
  --rc-background-surface: #1e293b;

  --rc-text-primary: #f8fafc;

  --rc-border-default: #334155;
}
```

Dark theme overrides semantic variables rather than component styles.

---

# D.34 High Contrast Theme

```css
[data-theme="high-contrast"] {
  --rc-background-default: #000000;

  --rc-text-primary: #ffffff;

  --rc-border-default: #ffffff;
}
```

High contrast themes improve accessibility for many users.

---

# D.35 Variable Inheritance

```text
Palette

↓

Semantic Variables

↓

Component Variables

↓

Component Styles

↓

Rendered UI
```

This layered approach simplifies maintenance.

---

# D.36 Theme Switching

```text
User Preference

↓

Theme Provider

↓

CSS Variables Updated

↓

Components Re-render

↓

UI Updated
```

Theme switching should require no component-specific logic.

---

# D.37 Variable Governance

Before introducing a variable:

✔ Existing variable evaluated

✔ Semantic naming applied

✔ Theme compatibility verified

✔ Documentation updated

✔ Cross-platform relevance confirmed

Variables should remain stable across releases.

---

# D.38 CSS Variable Anti-Patterns

Avoid

✘ Hardcoded hexadecimal values in components.

✘ Duplicate variable names.

✘ Platform-specific global variables.

✘ Component-specific global colors.

✘ Inline spacing values.

✘ Arbitrary z-index values.

✘ Deep variable dependency chains.

---

# D.39 Enterprise CSS Variable Checklist

Before publishing a new variable:

✔ Semantic purpose identified

✔ Naming convention followed

✔ Documentation completed

✔ Light theme implemented

✔ Dark theme implemented

✔ High contrast verified

✔ Accessibility reviewed

✔ Component adoption validated

---

# D.40 CSS Variable Philosophy

CSS Variables are more than implementation details.

They provide the runtime foundation for theming, accessibility, responsive design, and cross-platform consistency.

By centralizing visual decisions into a governed variable system, React Commerce enables rapid product evolution without sacrificing consistency, maintainability, or user experience.

---

# React Commerce Design Standard

> **Every visual style within React Commerce must resolve through documented CSS Variables derived from semantic design tokens. Variables should remain reusable, theme-aware, platform-independent, and governed as shared infrastructure, enabling consistent user experiences across every application, device, and future platform.**

---

# End of Appendix D — Enterprise CSS Variable Index & Theming Reference

---
# React Commerce Design System & Frontend Engineering Handbook

# Companion Reference Manual

# Appendix E — Enterprise React, TypeScript & Architectural Pattern Library

---

# E.1 Introduction

A successful enterprise application is built from repeatable engineering patterns rather than isolated implementations.

Patterns reduce:

- Cognitive load
- Code duplication
- Technical debt
- Architectural inconsistency

This appendix documents the official React Commerce implementation patterns.

Whenever engineers encounter a familiar problem, they should first consult this pattern library before creating a new solution.

---

# E.2 Pattern Categories

```text
React

↓

TypeScript

↓

Component

↓

State

↓

API

↓

Forms

↓

Performance

↓

Security

↓

Architecture

↓

Testing
```

Each category addresses a common engineering challenge.

---

# E.3 React Component Pattern

Recommended hierarchy

```text
Page

↓

Feature

↓

Container

↓

Section

↓

Reusable Component

↓

Primitive
```

Responsibilities become more generic toward the bottom.

---

# E.4 Smart vs Presentational Components

## Smart Components

Responsible for

✔ API calls

✔ State

✔ Business Logic

✔ Permissions

✔ Data Transformation

---

## Presentational Components

Responsible for

✔ Rendering

✔ Styling

✔ User Interaction

✔ Accessibility

✔ Layout

Never mix these responsibilities.

---

# E.5 Composition Pattern

Prefer

```tsx
<Card>

  <Card.Header />

  <Card.Body />

  <Card.Footer />

</Card>
```

Instead of

```tsx
<Card
  header
  footer
  showDivider
  compact
  horizontal
/>
```

Composition scales better than configuration.

---

# E.6 Compound Component Pattern

Useful for

```text
Tabs

Accordion

Modal

Select

Dropdown

Tree

Wizard
```

Each child component communicates through shared context.

---

# E.7 Provider Pattern

```text
<App>

↓

ThemeProvider

↓

AuthProvider

↓

WorkspaceProvider

↓

NotificationProvider

↓

Application
```

Providers expose shared application services.

---

# E.8 Custom Hook Pattern

Move reusable behavior into hooks.

Good

```tsx
useOrders()

useCustomers()

usePermissions()

useWorkspace()
```

Avoid duplicating side effects across components.

---

# E.9 Feature Folder Pattern

```text
orders/

├── api/

├── hooks/

├── components/

├── pages/

├── types/

├── validation/

├── services/

└── index.ts
```

Organize code by business capability.

---

# E.10 Barrel Export Pattern

Every folder should expose

```text
index.ts
```

Example

```ts
export * from "./Button";
```

Consumers should import from the feature root.

---

# E.11 TypeScript Type Pattern

Prefer

```ts
interface User {

}
```

for contracts.

Use

```ts
type
```

for

- Unions
- Mapped Types
- Utility Types

Use the most expressive construct.

---

# E.12 Domain Model Pattern

Separate

```text
Database Model

↓

API DTO

↓

Domain Model

↓

UI View Model
```

Avoid exposing database structures directly to the UI.

---

# E.13 API Client Pattern

```text
Component

↓

Hook

↓

Service

↓

HTTP Client

↓

API
```

Components should never communicate directly with HTTP libraries.

---

# E.14 Repository Pattern

Repository responsibilities

✔ Retrieve Data

✔ Cache

✔ Retry

✔ Normalize

✔ Error Handling

Repositories isolate infrastructure.

---

# E.15 Service Layer Pattern

Business rules belong inside services.

```text
OrderService

InventoryService

CampaignService

UserService
```

Services coordinate business workflows.

---

# E.16 Validation Pattern

Validation should exist in multiple layers.

```text
Client

↓

API

↓

Database
```

Never rely on a single validation layer.

---

# E.17 Form Pattern

```text
Form

↓

Validation Schema

↓

Controlled Components

↓

Submission

↓

Success State
```

Every form should define loading, validation, and success states.

---

# E.18 State Pattern

Separate

```text
Server State

↓

Application State

↓

Local UI State
```

Different problems require different state management strategies.

---

# E.19 Context Pattern

Use Context for

✔ Theme

✔ Authentication

✔ Localization

✔ Workspace

Avoid using Context for frequently changing large datasets.

---

# E.20 Error Boundary Pattern

```text
App

↓

Feature

↓

Error Boundary

↓

Recovery UI
```

Errors should remain localized.

---

# E.21 Loading Pattern

Preferred hierarchy

```text
Skeleton

↓

Partial Content

↓

Spinner
```

Loading experiences should reduce perceived latency.

---

# E.22 Pagination Pattern

```text
Request

↓

Page

↓

Response

↓

Append

↓

Render
```

Large datasets should never load all records simultaneously.

---

# E.23 Infinite Scroll Pattern

```text
Viewport

↓

Intersection Observer

↓

Next Page

↓

Append Results
```

Support manual loading when appropriate.

---

# E.24 Search Pattern

```text
User Input

↓

Debounce

↓

Validation

↓

API

↓

Results
```

Debouncing reduces unnecessary requests.

---

# E.25 Filter Pattern

Maintain

```text
Filters

↓

URL

↓

API

↓

UI
```

Filter state should remain shareable through URLs.

---

# E.26 Sorting Pattern

Sorting should be

✔ Stable

✔ Predictable

✔ Documented

Support ascending and descending order consistently.

---

# E.27 Optimistic Update Pattern

```text
User Action

↓

UI Update

↓

API

↓

Success

or

Rollback
```

Optimistic updates improve responsiveness.

---

# E.28 Cache Pattern

```text
Request

↓

Cache

↓

Fresh?

↓

Return

↓

Revalidate
```

Caching should be transparent.

---

# E.29 Retry Pattern

Retry only

✔ Network Errors

✔ Temporary Failures

Avoid retrying validation failures automatically.

---

# E.30 Permission Pattern

```text
User

↓

Role

↓

Permission

↓

Feature

↓

UI
```

Permissions should be enforced beyond the frontend.

---

# E.31 Feature Flag Pattern

```text
Feature Flag

↓

Evaluation

↓

Enabled?

↓

Render
```

Feature flags reduce deployment risk.

---

# E.32 Lazy Loading Pattern

Lazy load

✔ Pages

✔ Charts

✔ Editors

✔ Workflow Builders

✔ AI Modules

Initial bundles should remain small.

---

# E.33 Virtualization Pattern

```text
Large Dataset

↓

Viewport

↓

Visible Rows

↓

Render
```

Render only visible content.

---

# E.34 Event Pattern

```text
Action

↓

Event

↓

Subscribers

↓

Response
```

Loose coupling simplifies architecture.

---

# E.35 Dependency Injection Pattern

```text
Consumer

↓

Interface

↓

Implementation
```

Depend on abstractions.

---

# E.36 Adapter Pattern

Adapters isolate third-party services.

Example

```text
Stripe Adapter

Shiprocket Adapter

Meta Adapter

OpenAI Adapter
```

Swapping providers becomes significantly easier.

---

# E.37 Strategy Pattern

Example

```text
Discount Strategy

↓

Percentage

Fixed

Coupon

Campaign
```

Different algorithms share one interface.

---

# E.38 Factory Pattern

Factories create complex objects.

Example

```text
Notification Factory

↓

Email

SMS

WhatsApp

Push
```

Factories simplify construction logic.

---

# E.39 Observer Pattern

```text
Publisher

↓

Subscribers

↓

Notifications
```

Ideal for event-driven systems.

---

# E.40 Command Pattern

Represent actions as objects.

Examples

```text
Undo

Redo

Execute

Retry
```

Useful in workflow builders and editors.

---

# E.41 State Machine Pattern

```text
Draft

↓

Review

↓

Approved

↓

Published

↓

Archived
```

Finite state machines simplify workflow management.

---

# E.42 Pipeline Pattern

```text
Input

↓

Validation

↓

Transformation

↓

Business Rules

↓

Persistence

↓

Events
```

Pipelines create predictable processing flows.

---

# E.43 Testing Pattern

Recommended testing pyramid

```text
Unit Tests

↓

Integration Tests

↓

End-to-End Tests
```

Balance speed with confidence.

---

# E.44 Accessibility Pattern

Every component should verify

✔ Focus

✔ Keyboard

✔ Screen Reader

✔ Contrast

✔ Motion Preferences

Accessibility should be validated continuously.

---

# E.45 Documentation Pattern

Each feature should include

✔ README

✔ Architecture Diagram

✔ API Reference

✔ Examples

✔ Migration Notes

Documentation should evolve with the code.

---

# E.46 Performance Pattern

Measure

```text
Render

↓

Interaction

↓

Memory

↓

Network

↓

Bundle
```

Optimization without measurement is guesswork.

---

# E.47 Anti-Patterns

Avoid

✘ God Components.

✘ Massive Context Providers.

✘ Business logic inside UI.

✘ Deep prop drilling.

✘ Circular dependencies.

✘ Premature abstraction.

✘ Utility folders containing unrelated code.

✘ Anonymous types everywhere.

---

# E.48 Pattern Selection Matrix

| Problem | Recommended Pattern |
|----------|---------------------|
| Shared Behavior | Custom Hook |
| Shared State | Context / Store |
| Business Logic | Service Layer |
| External Provider | Adapter |
| Complex Object Creation | Factory |
| Multiple Algorithms | Strategy |
| UI Composition | Compound Component |
| Event Communication | Observer |
| Large Data | Virtualization |
| Workflow | State Machine |

---

# E.49 Enterprise Pattern Checklist

Before introducing a new pattern:

✔ Existing pattern evaluated

✔ Problem clearly identified

✔ Complexity justified

✔ Documentation written

✔ Examples included

✔ Testing completed

✔ Team consensus reached

Patterns should solve recurring problems—not create additional abstraction.

---

# E.50 Pattern Philosophy

Patterns are not goals in themselves.

They are proven solutions to recurring engineering problems.

An effective engineering organization favors a small, well-understood collection of patterns that are applied consistently rather than an ever-growing collection of clever abstractions.

The best pattern is the one that makes the code easier to understand, maintain, test, and evolve.

---

# React Commerce Design Standard

> **Every architectural decision within React Commerce should favor established, documented engineering patterns before introducing new abstractions. Patterns must improve clarity, maintainability, scalability, and consistency while remaining understandable to every engineer who joins the platform in the future.**

---

# End of Appendix E — Enterprise React, TypeScript & Architectural Pattern Library

# Appendix F — Enterprise Templates (ADR, RFC, PRD, Architecture Review, Code Review & Release Templates)

---

# F.1 Introduction

Templates reduce ambiguity.

Instead of reinventing documentation for every project, React Commerce uses standardized templates that make engineering communication:

- Consistent
- Searchable
- Reviewable
- Maintainable

Every significant engineering initiative should begin with one or more of these templates.

---

# F.2 Documentation Workflow

```text
Idea

↓

PRD

↓

RFC

↓

Architecture Review

↓

ADR

↓

Implementation

↓

Code Review

↓

Release

↓

Postmortem
```

Each artifact captures decisions at a different stage.

---

# F.3 Product Requirement Document (PRD)

A PRD explains **what** should be built and **why**.

Template

```text
# Product Requirement Document

Title

Owner

Date

Version

Status

Summary

Problem Statement

Business Goals

Success Metrics

Target Users

User Stories

Functional Requirements

Non-Functional Requirements

Out of Scope

Dependencies

Risks

Timeline

Acceptance Criteria
```

The PRD should avoid implementation details.

---

# F.4 Example User Story

```text
As a warehouse manager,

I want to scan incoming inventory,

So that stock updates automatically.
```

Every user story should express value.

---

# F.5 Functional Requirement Template

```text
Requirement ID

Description

Priority

Acceptance Criteria

Dependencies

Owner
```

Requirements should be testable.

---

# F.6 Non-Functional Requirements

Document

✔ Performance

✔ Security

✔ Accessibility

✔ Reliability

✔ Scalability

✔ Localization

✔ Compliance

These requirements are equally important.

---

```

Every major architectural decision deserves an ADR.

# F.10 ADR Example

ADR-014

Use TanStack Query

Status

Accepted

Reason

Server state synchronization
requires caching,
retry logic,
and background refresh.
```

Future engineers should understand the reasoning—not only the outcome.

# F.39 Documentation Anti-Patterns

Avoid

✘ Empty templates.

✘ Outdated documents.

✘ Missing ownership.

✘ Duplicate documentation.

✘ Implementation details inside PRDs.

✘ Architectural decisions without ADRs.

✘ Releases without rollback plans.

Documentation should remain living documentation.

---

# F.40 Enterprise Documentation Philosophy

Templates do not replace critical thinking.

They provide a shared structure that allows engineers, designers, product managers, QA engineers, and leadership to communicate effectively across teams and time.

Well-maintained documentation preserves organizational knowledge, accelerates onboarding, improves decision quality, and reduces costly misunderstandings.

The best documentation is concise, accurate, current, and actionable.

---

# React Commerce Design Standard

> **Every significant engineering initiative within React Commerce must be supported by standardized documentation. PRDs define product intent, RFCs encourage collaborative design, ADRs preserve architectural decisions, reviews validate quality, and release documentation ensures operational excellence. Documentation is a first-class engineering artifact and must evolve with the software it describes.**

# Appendix G — Enterprise Engineering Checklists & Operational Playbooks

---

# G.1 Introduction

Successful engineering organizations rely on repeatable execution rather than individual memory.

Operational checklists reduce:

- Human error
- Production incidents
- Deployment failures
- Security risks
- Inconsistent engineering practices

This appendix defines the official React Commerce operational playbooks.

Every engineer should use these checklists before major engineering activities.

---

# G.2 Engineering Lifecycle

```text
Plan

↓

Design

↓

Develop

↓

Review

↓

Test

↓

Deploy

↓

Monitor

↓

Improve
```

Each phase has mandatory verification steps.

---

# G.3 Feature Planning Checklist

Before implementation

✔ Business problem understood

✔ PRD approved

✔ User stories completed

✔ Success metrics defined

✔ Dependencies identified

✔ Technical risks documented

✔ Stakeholders aligned

✔ Acceptance criteria finalized

Never begin implementation with ambiguous requirements.

---

# G.4 Design Review Checklist

Verify

✔ Design approved

✔ Responsive layouts completed

✔ Accessibility reviewed

✔ Loading states designed

✔ Empty states designed

✔ Error states designed

✔ Dark theme reviewed

✔ Component reuse evaluated

Every screen should represent all possible user states.

---

# G.5 Architecture Checklist

Before coding

✔ Existing architecture reviewed

✔ Feature boundaries defined

✔ API contracts finalized

✔ Data models reviewed

✔ Error strategy documented

✔ Security implications evaluated

✔ Performance impact estimated

✔ Monitoring requirements identified

Architecture decisions should precede implementation.

---

# G.6 Development Checklist

Before opening a Pull Request

✔ Code builds successfully

✔ Lint passes

✔ Type checking passes

✔ Dead code removed

✔ Console logs removed

✔ Documentation updated

Code should be production-ready before review.

Treat components as reusable products.

Accessibility is a quality requirement.

# G.36 Daily Engineering Checklist

Each engineer should verify

✔ Current priorities understood

✔ Branch synchronized

✔ Tests passing

✔ Technical debt recorded

# G.37 Team Health Checklist

Engineering leadership should regularly evaluate

✔ Deployment frequency

✔ Build stability

✔ Incident trends

✔ Developer satisfaction

✔ Documentation quality

✔ Technical debt backlog

✔ Knowledge sharing

Healthy teams build healthy software.

---

# G.38 Operational Anti-Patterns

Avoid

✘ Deploying without rollback plans.

✘ Skipping code reviews.

✘ Ignoring monitoring alerts.

✘ Undocumented production changes.

✘ Manual production fixes without follow-up.

✘ Knowledge locked to one engineer.

✘ Treating incidents as isolated events.

Operational excellence depends on discipline.

---

# G.39 Enterprise Operations Philosophy

Checklists do not replace engineering judgment.

They ensure that routine but critical activities are consistently performed.

Operational maturity is achieved when quality becomes habitual rather than dependent on individual experience.

Reliable systems are built through disciplined execution repeated over time.

---

# React Commerce Design Standard

> **Every engineering activity within React Commerce must follow documented operational playbooks and quality checklists. Planning, development, testing, deployment, monitoring, incident response, and continuous improvement should be standardized, measurable, and repeatable to ensure reliable software delivery at enterprise scale.**

# React Commerce Design System & Frontend Engineering Handbook

# Appendix H — Enterprise Engineering Glossary, Index & Quick Reference

---

# H.1 Introduction

This appendix serves as the official glossary and quick reference for the React Commerce Engineering Handbook.

It provides:

- Common terminology
- Architectural definitions
- Design system vocabulary
- Engineering abbreviations
- Quick decision guides
- Alphabetical index

The goal is to establish a shared engineering language across every team.

---

# H.2 How to Use This Glossary

When encountering an unfamiliar engineering term:

1. Search alphabetically.
2. Read the official definition.
3. Refer to the related chapters for detailed guidance.
4. Use the standardized terminology in documentation, pull requests, RFCs, ADRs, and technical discussions.

Consistency in language improves consistency in architecture.

---

# H.3 Common Abbreviations

| Abbreviation | Meaning |
|--------------|---------|
| ADR | Architecture Decision Record |
| API | Application Programming Interface |
| ARIA | Accessible Rich Internet Applications |
| CI | Continuous Integration |
| CD | Continuous Delivery / Deployment |
| CRUD | Create, Read, Update, Delete |
| CSP | Content Security Policy |
| DTO | Data Transfer Object |
| DX | Developer Experience |
| E2E | End-to-End Testing |
| HTTP | Hypertext Transfer Protocol |
| JWT | JSON Web Token |
| KPI | Key Performance Indicator |
| LCP | Largest Contentful Paint |
| MFA | Multi-Factor Authentication |
| PR | Pull Request |
| PRD | Product Requirements Document |
| PWA | Progressive Web Application |
| RFC | Request for Comments |
| SDK | Software Development Kit |
| SLA | Service Level Agreement |
| SLO | Service Level Objective |
| SPA | Single Page Application |
| SSR | Server-Side Rendering |
| SSG | Static Site Generation |
| UI | User Interface |
| UX | User Experience |
| WCAG | Web Content Accessibility Guidelines |

---

# H.4 Engineering Glossary (A–Z)

## Accessibility

Designing software that can be used by people with diverse abilities, devices, and assistive technologies.

---

## Adapter

A software layer that converts one interface into another, allowing third-party services to integrate without affecting core business logic.

---

## API

A contract that allows software systems to communicate.

---

## Architecture

The high-level structure of software systems, defining responsibilities, dependencies, and interactions.

---

## Atomic Design

A methodology for building design systems from small reusable building blocks.

---

## Audit Log

A chronological record of significant system events for traceability and compliance.

---

## Breakpoint

A responsive layout threshold where UI behavior changes based on available screen width.

---

## Bundle

The compiled JavaScript and assets delivered to the browser.

---

## Caching

Temporarily storing data to improve performance and reduce repeated computation or network requests.

---

## Component

A reusable UI building block with a defined responsibility and API.

---

## Composition

Building complex interfaces by combining smaller reusable components.

---

## Context

A React mechanism for sharing state across component trees without extensive prop drilling.

---

## Design Token

A named design value representing colors, spacing, typography, motion, or other visual decisions.

---

## Domain Model

A representation of business concepts independent of infrastructure or presentation layers.

---

## Error Boundary

A React component that catches rendering errors within its child tree and displays a fallback UI.

---

## Feature Flag

A runtime mechanism for enabling or disabling functionality without redeploying code.

---

## Hook

A reusable React function that encapsulates stateful or side-effect logic.

---

## Hydration

The process of attaching React behavior to server-rendered HTML.

---

## Lazy Loading

Loading code or assets only when they are required.

---

## Memoization

Caching computation results to avoid unnecessary recalculation.

---

## Middleware

Software that executes between a request and its final handler, often used for authentication, logging, or validation.

---

## Observability

The ability to understand a system through logs, metrics, traces, and events.

---

## Pattern

A reusable solution to a recurring engineering problem.

---

## Primitive Component

The smallest reusable UI building block within the design system.

---

## Provider

A React component that exposes shared functionality through Context.

---

## Repository

An abstraction layer responsible for data access and persistence.

---

## Semantic Token

A design token named according to purpose rather than appearance.

---

## Server State

Data whose source of truth exists on the server.

---

## State Machine

A finite model describing valid transitions between predefined states.

---

## Storybook

A development environment for documenting and testing UI components in isolation.

---

## Theme

A collection of semantic visual values defining an application's appearance.

---

## Virtualization

Rendering only visible items from a large dataset to improve performance.

---

# H.5 React Terminology

| Term | Definition |
|------|------------|
| Component | Reusable UI element |
| Props | Input values for components |
| State | Mutable component data |
| Hook | Reusable behavior |
| Context | Shared state mechanism |
| Ref | Mutable object reference |
| Fragment | Grouping without extra DOM |
| Effect | Side-effect execution |
| Memo | Render optimization |
| Portal | Rendering outside the parent DOM tree |

---

# H.6 TypeScript Terminology

| Term | Definition |
|------|------------|
| Interface | Contract for object structure |
| Type Alias | Named type definition |
| Generic | Parameterized type |
| Enum | Named constant collection |
| Union | One of several possible types |
| Intersection | Combination of multiple types |
| Utility Type | Built-in type transformation |
| Literal Type | Exact value type |

---

# H.7 Accessibility Terminology

| Term | Meaning |
|------|---------|
| ARIA | Accessibility metadata |
| Focus Ring | Visible keyboard focus |
| Landmark | Semantic page region |
| Screen Reader | Assistive technology |
| Contrast Ratio | Text readability measurement |
| Reduced Motion | Accessibility preference for animation |

---

# H.8 Performance Terminology

| Metric | Description |
|---------|-------------|
| LCP | Largest Contentful Paint |
| CLS | Cumulative Layout Shift |
| INP | Interaction to Next Paint |
| TTFB | Time to First Byte |
| Bundle Size | Total shipped assets |
| FPS | Frames Per Second |

---

# H.9 Security Terminology

| Term | Definition |
|------|------------|
| Authentication | Verifying identity |
| Authorization | Verifying permissions |
| Encryption | Protecting data |
| Hashing | One-way transformation |
| CSRF | Cross-Site Request Forgery |
| XSS | Cross-Site Scripting |
| CSP | Content Security Policy |

---

# H.10 Architecture Decision Guide

```text
Need Shared UI?
↓

Component

Need Shared Logic?
↓

Hook

Need Business Rules?
↓

Service

Need Data Access?
↓

Repository

Need External Provider?
↓

Adapter

Need Shared State?
↓

Context or Store
```

Choose the simplest abstraction that solves the problem.

---

# H.11 Design Decision Guide

```text
Need Color?
↓

Semantic Token

Need Layout?
↓

Spacing Token

Need Typography?
↓

Font Token

Need Motion?
↓

Animation Token

Need Theme?
↓

Semantic Variables
```

Avoid hardcoded design values.

---

# H.12 Engineering Decision Guide

Before introducing something new, ask:

```text
Does this already exist?

↓

Can it be reused?

↓

Can it be simplified?

↓

Is it documented?

↓

Is it tested?

↓

Is it maintainable?
```

Reuse before creating.

---

# H.13 Repository Quick Reference

```text
apps/
    Applications

packages/
    Shared libraries

design-system/
    Design language

shared/
    Cross-cutting utilities

api/
    API clients

docs/
    Documentation

tests/
    Automated testing

scripts/
    Automation

infrastructure/
    Deployment
```

Every directory should have a clear purpose.

---

# H.14 Component Decision Tree

```text
Need Button?

↓

Button

Need Form?

↓

Form Components

Need Data?

↓

Table

↓

Chart

↓

List

Need Feedback?

↓

Toast

↓

Alert

↓

Dialog
```

Prefer existing components whenever possible.

---

# H.15 Code Review Quick Guide

Ask:

✔ Is it readable?

✔ Is it accessible?

✔ Is it secure?

✔ Is it tested?

✔ Is it documented?

✔ Is it maintainable?

✔ Is performance acceptable?

✔ Can it be simplified?

---

# H.16 Pull Request Quick Guide

A good pull request answers:

- Why was this change made?
- What changed?
- How was it tested?
- What are the risks?
- Are there screenshots?
- Is documentation updated?

---

# H.17 Release Readiness Checklist

```text
CI Passing

↓

Tests Passing

↓

Documentation Updated

↓

Monitoring Ready

↓

Rollback Ready

↓

Production Approval
```

Never skip readiness verification.

---

# H.18 Engineering Principles (Quick Reference)

1. Simplicity over cleverness.
2. Consistency over novelty.
3. Accessibility by default.
4. Performance through measurement.
5. Security throughout the lifecycle.
6. Documentation with implementation.
7. Composition over duplication.
8. Automation over repetition.
9. Platform thinking over isolated features.
10. Continuous improvement over perfection.

---

# H.20 Final Closing Statement

The React Commerce Design System & Frontend Engineering Handbook represents a comprehensive engineering foundation rather than a fixed destination.

Frameworks, tools, languages, and platforms will continue to evolve, but the enduring principles documented throughout this handbook—clarity, consistency, accessibility, security, performance, scalability, maintainability, collaboration, and continuous improvement—remain relevant regardless of technology.

Engineering excellence is achieved not through isolated moments of brilliance, but through disciplined application of sound principles, shared standards, thoughtful design, and relentless refinement.

Every contributor to React Commerce is encouraged to build upon these foundations, question assumptions respectfully, document decisions carefully, and leave the platform in a better state than they found it.

---

# React Commerce Engineering Charter

> **React Commerce is committed to building enterprise software that is understandable, accessible, secure, performant, scalable, extensible, observable, and maintainable. Every engineer is responsible for protecting these principles through thoughtful architecture, disciplined implementation, comprehensive documentation, collaborative review, and continuous learning. The long-term success of the platform depends not only on the software we create today, but on how effectively future teams can understand, extend, and improve it.**

---
